(<em>Copy. 3¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-167"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="167" data-folio="113" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n166_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 113">[p. 113]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Broach, August</span> 14, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 114).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward some goods asked for by the Governor of Broach. Complaint should be made of tolls exacted on cloth brought from ‘Oncleseare<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-147" href="#sec-05-fn-147" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-147" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ to Broach ‘only to bee whited’. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Ahmadābād, August</span> 16, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 114).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Discuss the advisability of detaining the Prince’s junk on her return from the Red Sea, which is shortly expected. They are themselves in favour of this course, as otherwise there is no prospect of their grievances being redressed. The opportunity is a good one, as the Surat merchants have, ‘besides there greate shippe abroade, whome they intend agayne for Judda, the <em>Gunjawer</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-148" href="#sec-05-fn-148" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-148" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> preparinge to send for Mocha, and a new small shipp a building, pretended for Shahir (?)<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-149" href="#sec-05-fn-149" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-149" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, and other of like burden nowe att Meselpatan, bound for Moca with pepper.’ They advise a further remittance, ‘wherein wee have observed your request of rup[ees] secaus coorah<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-150" href="#sec-05-fn-150" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-150" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>.’ Supply of indigo, calicoes, and sal-ammoniac. (<em>Copy.</em> 6 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Broach, August</span> 17, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 118).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They should take any stuffs they can get from Baroda, ‘in some measure to ease the burden of Barroch.’ Request the return of their broker. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to William Martin at Broach, August</span> 18, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 117).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have sent some morses’ teeth, as desired, for the Governor’s brother, but cannot furnish him with scarlet cloth, as there is none remaining. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-168"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="168" data-folio="114" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n167_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 114">[p. 114]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge to Is-hāq Beg, Governor of Surat</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> [20?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 118).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The expectation of the speedy arrival of the English fleet obliges them to remind him that the commands of the King and Prince for the provision of a suitable dwelling for them have not been obeyed, 'our goods and persones scarsly contayned in eight or nine severall howses, which beefore were in one.' (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the Factors at Broach</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 20, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 119).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They send bills for 6,000 mahmūdīs, and will forward 2,000 more in ready money to-morrow. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Ahmadābād</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> [21], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 117).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Promise an early remittance. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Broach</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 23, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 119).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward a supply of cash. They must not relax their efforts to obtain a full supply of calicoes. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to Giles James [at Burhānpur]</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 25, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 120).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Purchase of commodities. They have not sent any money, being themselves out of cash and about 25,000 mahmūdīs in debt; but they forward letters of credit 'from Virgevora [to] his sarvant Callian Shagger' [Kalyān Shāgird] to supply his wants. Pepper, ginger, and copper to be bought for Persia. He is to defer his departure as long as possible, in hope of the release of the caravan. Patterns of calico sent. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to [Augustine Spalding, George Ball, &amp;c., at Masulipatam?]</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 26, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 121).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their letters of July 20 and 23 [<em>see</em> p. 110], and have learnt with regret the troubles with the Dutch and the loss of the <em>Sun</em>. They note the advice not to provide any great quantity <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-169" data-page="169" data-folio="115" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n168_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 115">[p. 115]</button> of goods for the southwards this year, but the warning has come too late. If, however, Sir Thomas Dale and his council order the expected fleet to be sent on immediately to meet him at Priaman in October, as proposed, there will be no time for them to lade any goods. They doubt whether the Company will relish this disappointment. As to the supplies for the fleet. 'Our corrivals heare<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-151" href="#sec-05-fn-151" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-151" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, expectinge supply, have invested in clothinge for sowards, indico of both sortes, and some musters callicoes for there cuntrye all there cavidall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-152" href="#sec-05-fn-152" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-152" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, which if any come to fetch wee shall indeavore to effecte your ad[moni]cion in their seasure, though should far more rejoyce to be asertayned of some f[inal]le reconsiliacion and agreement att home to end these differences.' They doubt, however, the rumours as to the latter. The story of Queen Anne's death 'by the queenes infirmities is not unlike; if true [the King ? hath] lost a gracious and good lady'. Sir Walter Ralegh's end will 'finish his trouble, whose projecte was meerely contrived for his lyberty'. They relate their own troubles, which 'caused consideracion and allmost resolucion att returne of there juncke to expostulate these wronges by hir detencion att barr, which though itt would have caused us some trouble and [in] some respectes our hinderance, wee doubte not the evente would have releeved us in many particulers; which in regard of your generall differences (not [to] sett all in hazard) wee have wholly relinquished, to our longer sufferance in some, and such remedy in others as private giftes may induce'. The Portuguese have been loud in their threats of attacking the new fleet, but it is understood that they are obliged, by want of men and money, to wait until their Lisbon fleet arrives. (<em>Copy. 5 pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to William Methwold, &amp;c., at Masulipatam, August</span> 2[6], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 124).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thank them for their intelligence of the trouble with the Dutch, &amp;c. At Achin, Indian cloth has fallen much in price, and pepper is at the same time scarce. They note their wish for a direct supply of goods from England. Will in the meantime send on such commodities as are likely to vend at Masulipatam. As regards the return cargo, they remind them of 'our masters desire for increase of there lynnens returne, which (by sight of some <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-170" data-page="170" data-folio="116" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n169_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 116">[p. 116]</button> sortes from thence) wee perceave are better and cheaper then ours; besides they may in time no doubte assist to furnish the Red Sea trade, if prosecuted, which heare both by Pr[ince] and people is peremptorylie denied and forbidden us. Wee receaved your musters Bengala silke, which (though [you] distinguish nott) ar of two sortes, differinge in fineness [       ] more in cleare windinge. The better is something worse then the samples sent from England, whereof [wee] send you two skeynes hearewith. The fine is thatt [which] the Company exceptes agaynst and will none of itt; the [other?], if you can equall att the price in yours mencioned, and procure itts windinge in long skeynes (according to derecion) is the comodity required; wherein allso that you mistake nott the size, wee have sente you a skeyne wounde upp after the Persian forme, which is the length our masters desire, or att least three quarters thereof.’ A quantity should be sent to Surat by the junk of that place, if she remain long enough; if not, ‘by Cojanazans [Khwāja Nizām] convayance overland’; some specimens of various cloths should also be forwarded for transmission to England. ‘As for trade in Bengala, our masters have often required [the?] attempte, in expectacion, itt seemes, of some profitable comodities thence for England [<em>see</em> p. 50 <em>n.</em>]; butt, as the case standes, wee see not how it can as now be undertaken.’ The samples of indigo sent are very good, and the prices are cheaper than the ordinary Sarkhej indigo, which has of late years improved in price, while that of Biāna has fallen. The Company, however, complain that they are overstocked with indigo, and wish rather for linens. Goods for the southwards. Steel for Achin. The troubles with the Dutch. <em>P.S.</em>—‘There is very little difference twixt our ynch [and] the tessau [<em>see</em> p. 93 <em>n.</em>] of this place, thirty-seven whereof contay[ne] one English yarde.’ Request inquiry as to Bonner’s success at ‘Ekerree’ [<em>see</em> p. 56]. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 5½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to George Ball at Masulipatam, August</span> 27, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 124).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refers him for news to the public letter [of yesterday], which should be shown to Methwold. Begs instant advice if it be determined that the Surat fleet shall be summoned to meet the Bantam ships at Priaman. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-171"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="171" data-folio="117" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n170_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 117">[p. 117]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the</span> [<span class="sc">Commander of the English Fleet at Masulipatam</span>], <span class="sc">August</span> [30?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 127).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Prince Sultan Khurram has requested them to solicit that if an English ship be sent from Masulipatam to Surat it may come in company with his junk, now in the former port. They commend the request to his favourable consideration. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to Augustine Spalding</span>, &amp;<span class="sc">c., at Masulipatam, August</span> 30, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 127).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To the same effect as the foregoing. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, August</span> 31, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 128).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send copies of the letters from Masulipatam. The troubles with the Dutch 'will prevente prosecucion of our projected restrayninge the Princes shipp and inforce sufferance till more conveniensie to expostulate our wrongs'. Biddulph at Agra has been 'absolutely refused the Mocha trade, and slightly answered to all other the demands'. There is a difficulty in remitting them money; so they should borrow if possible. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Broach, August</span> 31, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 128).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cannot furnish them with money immediately; so they must take up some at interest or borrow of the brokers. This 'strainge unexpected innovacion' of war with the Dutch 'at sowards' will much hamper trade. They must push on with the preparation of the baftas, &amp;c., for England; and also take steps to recover the money owing at Baroda. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to Giles James at Burhānpur, September</span> 1, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 129).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward, for information and transmission to Agra, copies of the letters received from 'the Soward Councill', now at Masulipatam, announcing the quarrel with the Dutch. This news has caused the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-172" data-page="172" data-folio="118" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n171_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 118">[p. 118]</button> abandonment of the projected seizure of the Prince's junk. If Biddulph's efforts at court have not prevailed, James may inform the Khānkhānān that the Surat factors are willing to give ‘such securitie, eather of Moores or Banians, as himselfe shall require’ to answer Soares's claim at law, if the caravan be now released. They leave it to James to spend ‘any reasonable matter’ in bribes, if necessary. The ginger and pepper intended for Persia should not be bought, as ‘the Sowtharne accidentes’ have made the dispatch of a ship thither doubtful. The ‘byrames and serebaffes’ for the Red Sea should also be omitted, as that trade is ‘utterly refused us both by the Prince and these his ministers’. The goods for the southwards, however, should be hastened, as the fleet from England is expected shortly. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to [William Biddulph] at Agra, September</span> 1, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 130).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">He is no doubt aware of ‘the slender effectes the Prince his firmane prodused’, though apparently it was effectual enough in form; and they fear Soares will appeal to the Prince. If he does, Biddulph must plead that ‘wee are not liable to answere ought butt whatt consernes our masters busines’: that the English are forbidden to trade with the Portuguese: that Soares has no acknowledgement from Nicholas Bangham to show for the goods: and so on. He should protest strongly against the detention of the caravan as unnecessary, the English ‘havinge so great an estate in this cuntry to satisfie all demaunds’, and should complain of the loss caused to them by this procedure. If possible he should obtain ‘the Kinge or Prince his effectuall firmaen to prohibitte’ such action in future. He is also to urge satisfaction for the indigo stolen from a previous caravan. They note his proceedings for the recovery of the debts at court. The enclosed letters will give him the news of the troubles with the Dutch to the southwards. They are afraid the ‘Sotharne Councill’ will insist upon the new fleet from England joining them; if so, all idea of the dispatch of ships to the Red Sea and Persia must be abandoned, but they will endeavour to get permission to send home one vessel, probably the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-173" data-page="173" data-folio="119" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n172_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 119">[p. 119]</button> <em>Lion</em>. All Biddulph's efforts, therefore, should be devoted to the speedy release of the caravan. Errors in the invoice of goods. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 4 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to the Factors at Broach, September</span> [ ], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 132).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards by the bearers 3,000 mahmūdīs. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, September</span> [ ], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 132).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Are unable to send them money at present. Complaints from the shroffs. As regards the suggested dispatch of goods from Cambay to Surat by water, they must be cautious, as it is rumoured that the Portuguese 'frigatts armado of Dew' [Diu] have taken two Surat boats laden with timber from Nosārī. They find that conserves, &amp;c., are cheaper at Surat than at Ahmadābād. Sale of their lead. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to the Factors at Broach, September</span> [7], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 133).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Memorandum of the dispatch of bills of exchange. (¼ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the Factors at Broach, September</span> 8, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 133).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send a further supply of money. Their goods should be got ready for speedy dispatch. Thomas Sprage is to return to Surat at once, but he will be sent back shortly. Request from Masulipatam for the purchase of provisions for the fleet. They have been prevented from buying at Surat, and therefore request the factors at Broach to procure what they can. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell</span>] <span class="sc">at Surat to</span> [<span class="sc">Giles James</span>] <span class="sc">at Burhānpur, September</span> 10, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 134).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send Robert Tottle to be his linguist, attend the caravan, &amp;c. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-174"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="174" data-folio="120" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n173_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 120">[p. 120]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge to the Acting Governor</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-153" href="#sec-05-fn-153" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-153" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">of Surat</span>, <span class="sc">September</span> 11, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 135).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The English have been prohibited by the Camongoo<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-154" href="#sec-05-fn-154" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-154" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> from purchasing wheat, &amp;c., for their ship, which is expected from the Red Sea and is then to prepare for a voyage to Pr[iaman?]. They request the removal of this embargo, or the assignment of a reason, since the right of provisioning has been granted by the King's and Prince's farmāns. If he distrusts their intentions, he may lock up the door of their warehouse. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to 'the Bantam Councill att Musellapatam', September</span> 11, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 135).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They would be glad to hear whether the Council persist in requiring the dispatch of the fleet to their assistance. An endeavour has been made to purchase the provisions they have asked for, but the Acting Governor has, on some foolish suspicion, prohibited this pending the Governor's return. As regards powder, the Dutch have forty barrels, saved from the wreck of their vessel [<em>see</em> p. 29 <em>n.</em>], but it is doubtful whether they will sell. (<em>Copy. Part illegible.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to the Factors at Masulipatam, September</span> 11, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 136).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refers to the accompanying letters [<em>see preceding entry</em>] for news. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to Is-hāq Beg, Governor of Surat, September</span> 14, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 137).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">News has arrived that at the Prince's command the Khānkhānān has released the caravan, and that orders have also been issued to compensate the English for the robbery of their indigo. He reminds the Governor that they have hitherto forborne to complain regarding his non-provision of a suitable dwelling for them, and hopes that steps to content them will be taken immediately. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-175"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="175" data-folio="121" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n174_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 121">[p. 121]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to the Factors at Broach</span>, September 15, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 137).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward bills for 3,000 mahmūdīs. Will send back Thomas Sprage to-morrow. The goods at Burhānpur have been released and are now on their way to Surat. The Governor on his return licensed their purchases, and they have consequently bought 2,000 maunds of wheat. They will, however, still need some from Broach. Gravances <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-155" href="#sec-05-fn-155" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-155" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, ‘if you meane cheine <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-156" href="#sec-05-fn-156" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-156" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>,’ can probably be bought as cheap at Surat. ‘White jaller <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-157" href="#sec-05-fn-157" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-157" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, beeinge a kind of small pease, and c[ ]wlee, a kind of red much used and desired by our people,’ should be provided ; also butter, which may be obtained from Ankleswar, ‘where itt is much made.’ (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to the Factors at Broach</span>, September 16, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 138).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Purchase of provisions and making of biscuits. Thomas Sprage, the bearer, will deliver them 2,000 mahmūdīs. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Ahmadābād</span>, September 17, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 138).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Recommends the bearer, a native merchant, for the conveyance of any goods they may wish to send to Surat. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell</span>] <span class="sc">at Surat to William Biddulph, &amp;c.</span>, [<span class="sc">at Agra</span>], September 17, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 138).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received his letter with copy of the Prince’s second farmān to the Khānkhānān, who, on receiving the original, ‘gave presente order for a full restitucion without further delay or question,’ and also issued two farmāns, one for compensation to the English for the indigo taken from them, the other securing them ‘quiett and secure passage’ throughout his district. They praise Biddulph for obtaining these satisfactory results. As regards the Prince’s ‘absolute deniall’ of the Red Sea trade, they have resolved, ‘seeinge there is no very greate hope wee shall use any meanes of constraynt,’ to prepare a petition to the Prince setting forth their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-176" data-page="176" data-folio="122" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n175_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 122">[p. 122]</button> reasons. They propose to stay the junk on arrival for a few days to extort, if possible, some redress of their grievances, but will not act in earnest 'untill our re-establishment att sowards'. If the detention of his ship be brought to the Prince's knowledge, 'you may impute [it] to the pride and obstinacy of his ministers, thatt will not performe his commands, though sufficiently ample.' They are glad to hear of the sale of the camlets and mohairs. Disposal of cloth and provision of indigo. 'Itt is rumorde heare thatt the Prince C[horoo]m is disgraced by his father; which were much to be desired, for so longe as the master is not our favorer, his servantes will little befrind us.' The Portuguese threatened to occupy Swally Road before the fleet arrived, but 'certayne intilligence affirmeth they cannott come soe soone, wantinge both men and mony'. If they come at all, it will not be much before December. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to John Bangham at BurhĀnpur, September</span> 19, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 141).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have sent Robert Tottle with 'perwans' from the late Partab Shāh to facilitate the passing of goods at 'Dayta' [<em>see p.</em> 74 <em>n.</em>]. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to John Bangham at BurhĀnpur, September</span> 25, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 141).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advise dispatch of Tottle, who will see to the clearing of the first caravan at Dhāita, 'which, as itt is reported, is likly to bee suddanly possessed by Chan Chanas sarvantes, and therefore hope itt will passe the easier by virtue of his perwane.' Both the latter and Partab Shāh's parwāna will then be sent to Bangham. No news yet of the ships from England or of the <em>Lion</em>. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to Robert Tottle, September</span> 25, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 142).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Directions for clearing the caravan and sending the Khānkhānān's parwāna to Burhānpur. Money forwarded for his expenses. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-177"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="177" data-folio="123" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n176_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 123">[p. 123]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, September</span> 25, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 140).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They cannot yet decide whether a ship can be spared for Persia. Release of the goods detained at Burhānpur. No news yet of the fleet or of the <em>Lion</em>. They are awaiting directions from Masulipatam or from home. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to William Biddulph, &amp;c., [at Agra?], September</span> 30, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 142).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No tidings yet of the fleet from England nor of the <em>Lion</em> from the Red Sea. Rumours of Khurram's disgrace. 'The [     ]sherses lyberty is devulged, and thatt great some [s     ] and mony have beene presented him by the [King?     ] raysinge of him will be the fall of the other [     ] whose high expectacion would [by no means be?] induced to conformitie.' As regards the goods desired by Āsaf Khān, they will gladly comply if he will send his servants to the fleet to fetch them. It is probable, however, that the Prince's servants will refuse to allow any such goods to pass, as Khurram was very angry recently at their letting slip the picture of a horse which Biddulph presented to the King [<em>see</em> p. 111], and ordered them to be more careful in future; 'the Governor of Barroch with a raged regiment beeinge allready heare arrived under pretence thereof, though wee knowe this machivill<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-158" href="#sec-05-fn-158" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-158" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> divell, Isake Beage, hath wrought itt rather to confirme his pretended suspecte of the danger the towne is in duringe the time of our shippes, if not stored with soldiers, whereof he hath none.' Transcripts of their former letters sent. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Factors at Broach, October</span> [2], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 143).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have been prevented from sending 'dubas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-159" href="#sec-05-fn-159" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-159" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>' for the butter. News has just come from Gandevi that three vessels are in sight, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-178" data-page="178" data-folio="124" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n177_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 124">[p. 124]</button> ‘whereof two are crosse sayles and the third the juncke, either of this place or Goga.’ The same intelligence just received from Nosārī. They conjecture that the two ‘crosse sayles’ are the <em>Lion</em> and her pinnace. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Commander of the English Ships at Surat Bar</span>, OCTOBER 2, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 143).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Customer (?), being about to send a boat to inquire news of the Prince’s junk, has desired a note to request the English commander to give what information he can. He may do this, but should not permit any one to land until the factors come aboard, which they hope to do this evening in Swally Road. The junk should be kept at Surat Bar for the present. There is no danger of attack from the Portuguese. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">HENRY CROSBY’S JOURNAL OF HIS VOYAGE<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-160" href="#sec-05-fn-160" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-160" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> IN THE <em>CHARLES</em> TO SURAT, MARCH–OCTOBER, 1619 (<em>Marine Records</em>, xxix, 1–8).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Passed the Lizard March 21. Reached ‘Souldany baye’ [Table Bay] June 24. While there ‘the Daynes came in with six sayle’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-161" href="#sec-05-fn-161" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-161" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Bickley’s ships sailed for Surat on July 12. Refreshed at the Comoro Islands. Arrived at Swally October 3. (9 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">CAPTAIN JOHN BICKLEY’S NOTES OF HIS VOYAGE FROM THE CAPE TO SURAT [JULY–OCTOBER, 1619] (<em>O. C.</em> 806).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-162" href="#sec-05-fn-162" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-162" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Passed ‘the Cape of Agulias’ July 17 [1619]. Reached Mohilla [Comoro Islands] August 18. The <em>Charles</em> alone had 97 men sick, most of whom recovered their health at this place. Their anchorage was off ‘Mirengona’ [Miringoni]. Left August 23 and arrived at Swally October 3. (<em>Holograph.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-179"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="179" data-folio="125" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n178_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 125">[p. 125]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to the Factors at Broach, October</span> 6, 1619 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2122, <em>f.</em> 144).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The ships from England have arrived. The news they brought will be communicated later; ‘meane time you may apprehend [that] there is no allteracion of what Sir Thomas Roe [? enjoy]ned, butt the Companies whole affayres, both for land and sea, confirmed to our disposure.’ They request early dispatch of goods and provisions. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Commander of the Fleet, October</span> 6, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 144).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Inquire for certain letters from England. As to purchase of provisions and choice of a purser to look after this. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to [the Factors on Board the Ships ?], October</span> [6], 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 144).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As soon as accommodation can be prepared for them, the coaches will be sent to fetch them up; ‘in which intrim do intreate your patience and that you speake unto the Comander not to permitte any of his people to straggle abroade.’ Inquire for letters addressed to any of the factors in India or Persia. (<em>Copy. Damaged. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">[<span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James] at Surat to the Factors at Ahmadābād, [October</span> 7], 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 146).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrival of the <em>Charles, Ruby</em>, and <em>Diamond</em>. ‘The cheife master, who also comanded the rest, was John Bickle, that was a mate in the <em>Guift</em> att your [Browne’s] comming forth from England.’ Their consorts, the <em>Palsgrave, Elizabeth</em>, and <em>Hope</em>, have gone for Bantam. At the Cape they met the <em>Little James</em> homeward bound. They found there some letters left by Sir Thomas Roe, but they were too wet and rotten to be read. ‘The expected passificacion twixt the Dutch and us is nott (itt seemes) concluded, nor do the Company <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-180" data-page="180" data-folio="126" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n179_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 126">[p. 126]</button> advise ought conserning itt. Some of the States<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-163" href="#sec-05-fn-163" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-163" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> were in England att departure of these shippes, and greatly blamed by the Kinge, yett no conclucion, though the expectacion therof d[etai]ned the whole fleets dispatch till Mar[ch, w]hereby they tucht not att Succatora.' No news yet of the <em>Lion</em> or of the Prince's junk. The Company's letter will show how the fleet is to be disposed of, and the large supply they have ordered for Persia. Any goods provided at Ahmadābād for that purpose should be sent down at once. As matters stand, the investment ordered by the Company for Bantam must be held over, but that for Sumatra should be provided in full. The Company's opinion regarding indigo and desire for increase of all linen commodities should be noted. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 2 pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to William Biddulph, &amp;c., [at Agra], October</span> 7, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 147).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrival of the fleet. Copy of the Company's letter sent herewith. The <em>Lion</em> has not yet returned. Already they have had great inquiry for 'tophas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-164" href="#sec-05-fn-164" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-164" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>', but they are hindered by the officers' 'accustomed musseroes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-165" href="#sec-05-fn-165" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-165" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>'. They urge him to take every opportunity of disgracing the latter, 'espetially to Isake Beage, whose innate hatred debarreth us all meanes of negosiacon, not only in deprivinge us of fitting houses, butt allso molestinge us with prohibicions in [all] the passages of our businesses.' They send a petition for presentation to the Prince. Provision of silk. The Company's complaints regarding the quality of the semians and also of the way in which they were packed. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Petition to Prince Khurram (Shāh Jahān), October</span> 7, 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 145).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'Peticion of the humble supplyante Thomas Kerridge and Company, English merchantes resident in Surat, who, prostrated with <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-181" data-page="181" data-folio="127" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n180_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 127">[p. 127]</button> due and accustomed reverence, do with all humillitie peticion the high, mightie, vertuous and resplendant Prince, master of the world<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-166" href="#sec-05-fn-166" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-166" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, and for whose increase of felicitie wee allwayes pray.’ After acknowledging the Prince’s favour in releasing the caravan, they complain of the prohibition of the English trade with the Red Sea. They recall the rescue of the <em>Begum Shāhī</em> from the pirates<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-167" href="#sec-05-fn-167" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-167" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and the help afforded last year by the English sailors when the Prince’s junk went aground. ‘Profitt wee seeke nott;’ but they cannot send ships to convoy the junks unless allowed to recoup themselves by trade for the expense. ‘Every yeare in many occacions wee do Your Highness service, whereof itt seemes you have not receaved informacion nor wee the musero’ [<em>see</em> p. 126 <em>n.</em>]. They further beg assistance in the recovery of a debt from ‘one Groo’ [Guru], the removal of the embargo on purchases at Nosārī and Baroda, and permission to return to their former factory, which is still unoccupied, while they are forced to make shift with eight houses, not one of which is convenient. If this cannot be permitted, they solicit licence to build a suitable dwelling. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to the Factors at Masulipatam, October</span> 8, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 148).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Express astonishment at not having heard from ‘the remooved Bantam Co[uncil]’ whether they still insist that the ships from England should join them at Priaman. Arrival of the fleet. No pacification yet concluded with the Dutch. The Company wish for larger linen investments and less indigo. It has been decided to send the whole fleet to Persia, as the Company have advised the dispatch of four carracks and six galleons from Lisbon this year. Provision of raw silk [from Bengal?]. ‘The arrived fleete [met] att the Cape with six shipps belonginge to [the] Kinge of Denmark, thatt (as themselves reported) were bound for Sealon with weomen and [     ].’ (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 3½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to William Martin at Broach</span>, [OCTOBER 8?], 1619 (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 154).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To dispatch immediately all goods intended for Persia. Those from Burhānpur have already arrived. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-182"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="182" data-folio="128" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n181_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 128">[p. 128]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to Captain Bickley at Swally, October</span> [8 ?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 150).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Letters received. Berthing of the <em>Diamond</em>. Provisions for the fleet. (<em>Copy. Damaged. <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to</span> [THE <span class="sc">Bantam Council</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 9, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 151).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrival of ships from England. Copies of the letters brought by the fleet have been sent by way of Masulipatam. Disposal of the ships. The <em>Lion</em> is not yet returned from the Red Sea. The Company having sent a large supply of money and goods for Persia, it has been resolved to order the whole fleet thither. (<em>Copy. Part illegible. 1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to William Martin and Richard Lancaster</span> [AT <span class="sc">Broach</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 9, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 154).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They are to refrain for the present from passing any goods over the river, but to house them securely and await orders. (<em>Copy. Damaged. <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to</span> [<span class="sc">Captain Martin Pring</span> ?], <span class="sc">October</span> 9, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 151).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thanks him for his letter. Arrival of the fleet, which left the Downs March 16. News brought by the ships. Their intended dispatch to Persia. The <em>Lion</em> has not yet returned from Mocha. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to</span> [<span class="sc">Captain John Bickley at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 10, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 152).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As to the discharge of his cargoes and purchase of provisions for the ships. They beg him to send some rials by the merchants. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Merchants arrived in the Fleet, October</span> 10, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 153).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have now, as promised, sent a couple of coaches to convey them to Surat. To avoid trouble in the custom-house they should bring <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-183" data-page="183" data-folio="129" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n182_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 129">[p. 129]</button> as little luggage as possible. Captain Bickley has been asked to send some rials in their charge. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to</span> [<span class="sc">Captain Bickley</span>?] <span class="sc">at Swally, [October</span> 10? 1619] (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 154).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Hopes to be at Swally to-night and to arrive on board in the morning. In the meantime the rials and coral should be prepared for delivery. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to</span> [<span class="sc">William Martin at Broach</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 12, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 154).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Understands from his letter that the Cambay goods had already passed the river before the arrival of the order to stop them [<em>see p.</em> 128]. Payment of the customs. They await the commodities destined for Persia. Bills for 3,000 mahmūdīs sent. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">The Same</span>?] <span class="sc">to Messrs. Ewing</span> (?) <span class="sc">and Dyer</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-168" href="#sec-05-fn-168" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-168" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> [<span class="sc">on board the Fleet at Swally</span>?], <span class="sc">October</span> [13?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 155).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The officers refuse to permit the passage of the coral and the [rials?]; so he is forced to send both back again. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to John Browne, &amp;c., at Ahmadābād, October</span> [18?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 155).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The indigo caravan, were not the goods for Persia included, might with advantage have been deferred for two months, as it is difficult to find warehousing room at Surat. The goods from Cambay two days ago ‘came safely to Beriewe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-169" href="#sec-05-fn-169" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-169" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, and [are] there howsed att the Adowyas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-170" href="#sec-05-fn-170" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-170" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’. Both the dispatch of the Surat fleet to Priaman and the supply of provisions have been countermanded; ‘for first the requyrie was lame, neather strengthened by Sea Councill nor seconded, though both were promised’; secondly, the Company have expressly commanded the prosecution of the [Persian?] trade; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-184" data-page="184" data-folio="130" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n183_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 130">[p. 130]</button> thirdly, the seamen declare they cannot reach Priaman by the time fixed. Goods for Persia and Sumatra. They have landed the rials, but cannot yet procure bills for transmission. The <em>Lion</em> has arrived from the Red Sea, having lost but one man, and sold her cargo 'to contente'. Trouble at the custom-house over the coral brought by the fleet. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">The Same</span>] <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">William Martin, &amp;c., at Broach?</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 18, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 156).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Goods and letters received. As regards the investments, they are 'by all possible meanes to inlarge those enjoyned for England, both in narrowe and broad baftes, accordinge to order receaved from our masters, where more are required then this cuntry will afforde'. Troubles with the officials. Money forwarded. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to John Browne, &amp;c., at Ahmadābād, October</span> [19?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 157).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have got their specie cleared from the custom-house, on undertaking to reship the coral if so commanded by the Prince. In the meantime they have housed the coral at 'Raneell<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-171" href="#sec-05-fn-171" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-171" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, the house chaptt<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-172" href="#sec-05-fn-172" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-172" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> both by them and us'. The Mokha factors calculate they have made nearly a hundred per cent. clear profit. This trade is too good to be lost, and they will further consider the means of prosecuting it. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to William Biddulph, &amp;c., [at Agra], October</span> 19, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 157).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Lion</em> arrived on the 14th, having been [fifty]-four days on her return voyage. This was partly due to their waiting too long for the Prince's junk, which, like that of Goghā, has lost her monsoon and cannot return till next year. Further troubles with the officials. The latter forbade the landing of the coral from the fleet, and would not permit it to be passed over the river. Thereupon the English <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-185" data-page="185" data-folio="131" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n184_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 131">[p. 131]</button> refused to pass over their silver, threatening to return both to the ships. [<em>Part illegible.</em>] They beg him to represent their grievances and to submit the enclosed petitions. Presents, &amp;c., for the Prince. ‘To Asaphe Chan you may allso alleadge the cause of our not bringinge tofne [<em>see</em> p. 126 <em>n.</em>], and resolucion not to bringe anye more till assured remedy; for whome wee will send the pearle mencioned in our masters letters, in hope both of his payment and frendshippe.’ Other goods will be forwarded to him as soon as possible. It will probably be necessary to send the whole fleet to Jask. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Petition from the Surat Factors to Afzal Khān, October</span> 20, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 159).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their ships have brought a quantity of coral, ‘which the Governor and merchantes, consultinge together, prohibitt us to sell, and have written a joynt peticion unto the Prince Shaugh Jehaune His Highnesse for his nishane [<em>see</em> p. 107 <em>n.</em>] to forbid itts sale and our further trade in thatt comodity, pretending our dealinge therein to bee prejudiciall unto them and His Highnesse.’ But the latter would benefit by the increase of the customs paid by the English, while the loss to the merchants cannot be great, seeing that much coral is already imported from the Red Sea and Christendom, ‘all which is yearely spent in the cuntry of Decann.’ They beg him to use his influence with the Prince to redress this grievance, and further to procure them liberty to trade to Mokha, in which case they would be able to preserve the Prince’s ship from pirates. They hear that this year several vessels have come ‘belonginge to a cuntry called Denmark’; their intentions are doubtful, and it would be well to safeguard ‘both his and your Honors shippes’. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Petition from the Surat Factors to the Prince</span>, [OCTOBER 20, 1619] (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 160).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They recite their grievances and pray redress. They mention that their ship from Mokha nearly lost her monsoon in waiting to convoy his junks, hoping thus ‘to have manifested our service to Your Highness’. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-186"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="186" data-folio="132" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n185_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 132">[p. 132]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to</span> [<span class="sc">Robert</span>] <span class="sc">Young</span> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 22, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 160).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards some bows and arrows sent from Ahmadābād by Browne as a present to Young. Kerridge has appeased the Governor of ‘Urpall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-173" href="#sec-05-fn-173" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-173" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ and the sailors may now obtain water from thence. Requests that some [strong waters?] may be sent up. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to the Commander and Masters of the Fleet, October</span> 22<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-174" href="#sec-05-fn-174" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-174" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 160).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They entrust the fitting up of the pinnace to Captain Bickley. [          ] Cur[tis]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-175" href="#sec-05-fn-175" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-175" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> appointed master of her. [John] Weddell<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-176" href="#sec-05-fn-176" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-176" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> appointed a member of the Sea Council. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to William Martin at Broach, October</span> 22, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 161).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has arranged for housing the goods brought by the caravan, except those for Persia, which must be embarked at once, as the fleet is to sail shortly. The purchase of ‘linnens’ should be pushed on as fast as possible. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge</span>] <span class="sc">at Surat to Captain Bickley at Swally, October</span> [28?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 161).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Protests his sincerity. Goods sent and required. Preparation of the fleet for the voyage to Persia. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to the Captain of the Guards</span> [<span class="sc">at the watering-place?</span>], <span class="sc">October</span> 28, 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 162).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Communicates some complaints and requests investigation. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-187"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="187" data-folio="133" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n186_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 133">[p. 133]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge at Surat to John Browne, &amp;c., at Ahmadābād, October</span> [28?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 162).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrival and disposal of the goods for Persia, &amp;c. A letter has been received from a shroff named 'Natashawe' [<em>see</em> p. 91], now at Ahmadābād, offering to supply the English merchants there with money, to be repaid at Surat. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge and Thomas Rastell at Surat to William Martin, &amp;c., at Broach</span>, [NOVEMBER 1?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 168).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have acquainted the Governor of Broach with the dispute regarding the customs, and he has sent a parwāna ordering the Customer to treat the English properly. This is forwarded herewith; also 18,000 mahmūdīs in bags under the care of certain Englishmen, besides some bills of exchange. <em>P.S.</em>—A further 'perwanna' received from 'Hemett Chan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-177" href="#sec-05-fn-177" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-177" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>', blaming the Customer for his 'ruffe dealinge'. 'The Mirza<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-178" href="#sec-05-fn-178" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-178" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>' has also written to the latter, ordering him to leave the payment of the customs to Kerridge. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Instructions to Captain Bickley for a Voyage to Persia, November</span> [3?], 1619 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 173).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To sail to the port of Jask and there land the goods placed on board the fleet. To be vigilant against attacks by the Portuguese. Directions as to any prizes that may be taken. Limit of stay at Jask. If no risk from the Portuguese, the <em>Ruby</em> and the pinnace may be left a few days longer to take in goods. The <em>Lion</em> is placed under the orders of Captain Bickley<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-179" href="#sec-05-fn-179" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-179" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, but her officers are not to be removed without Weddell's consent. She is to rank as the third ship of the fleet. 'Our masters, havinge beene exceedingly [abu]sed by some mens excessive expence in fresh victualls, have now ordayned that you shall not exceed three or four meales a weake.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-180" href="#sec-05-fn-180" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-180" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> [<em>Part illegible.</em>] If on the return of the fleet (having <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-188" data-page="188" data-folio="134" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n187_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 134">[p. 134]</button> left the <em>Ruby</em> and the pinnace behind) they find Swally occupied by the Portuguese in force, it would be well to await the arrival of those two ships before making any attack. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 3½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to the Company, November</span> 3 <span class="sc">and</span> 5, 1619 (<em>O. C.</em> 818).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-181" href="#sec-05-fn-181" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-181" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to their previous letters of February 16 and March 12. The ivory brought by Bonner's fleet was detained by the custom-house officials over four months, to their great loss. Their lead was also detained until two months ago, which has frustrated the year's sale; they are further hampered by 'itts transportinge to other parts beinge prohibited, and of late more narrowlie looked unto'. However, of both these commodities the quantities formerly advised should be yearly sent, as also sixty chests of tin. 'Your tapestrie &amp;c. mercerie wares, after longe lyinge in the customhowse, were sealed by the Princes officers and sent to their master at court, where also after certaine monethes detention beinge opened, the Prince tooke choice of what he liked and appointed part of the rest to the Kings usse; which beinge yet unpaid for, we cannott certainelie advize the benefitt that will arise thereon.' Some more should be sent for presents, according to the list enclosed; also forty or fifty pieces of satin of good colours. The commodities listed by Sir Thomas Roe for the lading of the <em>Lion</em> are nearly ready; 'onlie in the lynen comodities we have bene verie much backwarded by the Governour and merchants of this place, whoe by all possible meanes have and doe still endeavour to prevent any large investment therein.' The indigo caravan from Agra arrested by Soares; it has now been released at a cost of above 150<em>l.</em> charges, besides other damages. 'Presentlie uppon disspeede of the junke of this place for the Redd Sea, by whome we sent our letters of the 12th March, these merchants, falinge into consideration of theire owne and our bussines, perceiveing we began in severall places our lynen investmentts, caled together all the brokers of this towne and parts adjacent, straightlie <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-189" data-page="189" data-folio="135" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n188_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 135">[p. 135]</button> charginge them under great penalties not to buy, sell or deale for the English in any comoditie whatsoever, forceinge writeinges from such as had formerlie dealt for us, by greater feare to restraine them ; and not contented by abridginge us this place and Nunceree, wrote letters also to the Governours of Baroch and Brodera that it was the Prince his pleasure we should not buy any linen comodities ; to the latter of whome, haveinge never before had any residence in his towne, we sent the Kinge and Princes firmaens in contradiction, which were of no validitie soe longe as the Governour [of] Surrat req[uired the] contrarie ; not for that he is of more power then any of them, but because he pretended to have the Prince his authoritie for his order. Thereuppon, after sundrie conferrences and publicke protests on both parts, an assemblie of all the merchants were convocated by the Governour, where both in the Prince his name and for themselves they publicklie protested against our trade into the Redd Sea, vowinge never to suffer it, nor any other if wee immediatelie gave it not over or had the least intendment thither ; and fynallie prohibitted our lynen investments in all places on this side Amadavad, confineinge our employmentts, both for England and southwards, to Baroch onlie, which, as it is the place of common recourse for all men, soe is it indeede the dearest of all other ; the rest, as Surratt, Nunceree, and Brodra, they reserve for their owne investments, which they alleadge afford not comodities sufficient to lade the Prince his shippe for the Redd Sea, and therefore required the withdrawinge of our moneie ; which, soe great a necessitie (as there stoppinge the whole course of our busines) compellinge, we condescended unto, and promised not to make any investments for the Redd Sea untill the Prince his pleasure were further knowne ; whoe uppon our requirie beinge therein solicited by Wm. Biddulph (your agent at court) hath two severall times by his owne mouth given absolute denyall ; whereby untill Your Worships shall ordaine and authorize a remedie, you are utterlie frustrated of that trade. Our dwellinge howse, by vertue of a firmaun sent downe before Sir Thomas Roe his departure (the contracted time of three yeares being expired), was also taken from us, and we exposed to sundrie pettie habitations, whereby our troubles are greatlie encreased, Your Worships damnified in sundrie kindes, and busynes protracted ; for which as yet, it beinge now <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-190" data-page="190" data-folio="136" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n189_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 136">[p. 136]</button> five monthes since, we finde no relieffe, there beinge indeede no convenyent howse in all the towne to be procured, for rent, except neare the river side, where they will not permitt us to dwell; and to buy or build we were three yeares since by the Prince his expresse comande forbidden.' Letters received on August 25 from the Bantam Council announcing their arrival at 'Narrapellie' [<em>see</em> p. 110], and from the Masulipatam factory advising that Dale had reached that place with his fleet. Copies of these letters forwarded. Bickley's fleet arrived on the 2nd [October]. News from the Cape; where 'the 12th ditto [July] arrived also five Danish shipps, whoe, though they pretended to be bound for Ceilon, to inhabitt, it is to be feared they have some more facill designe'. Their business has been much retarded by first the Bantam Council's demand that the fleet should be sent to their assistance, and, secondly, the delay in the arrival of the ships, which made them doubt whether any were coming this year. 'The <em>Lyon</em>, whoe the last yeare was sent to trade into the Redd Sea, stayinge over longe for dispatch at Mocha, returned not hither untill the 14th current<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-182" href="#sec-05-fn-182" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-182" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, haveinge bene 54 daies on the way and had almost lost her monsone (as both the junks of this place and Goga have done, for [they] are not returned from Judda); whose voyadge, for the quantitie of goods shee carried, hath returned to good accompt. The English cloth (soe much as was merchantable) sould at Synan to good profitt, where 20 or 30 clothes may yearlie finde vent; but the comodities of this place would indeede give liffe to that trade, all sent beinge sould at cent. per cent. profitt, and the returnes in readie specie; whereof in our next per sea we shalbe more large, and wish you may be pleased to consider and determine on the course we shall ellswhere propose for itts contynuance. Of the specie and comodities come on this your fleete we have here landed the proportion by Your Worships designed, and left the remainder aboard intended for Persia, as by you enordered; wherewith for more saftie (in regard of a rumour of four gallions gon for Ormus) we have determined the whole fleete for Jasques. In the dischardginge of your goods here we landed first your specie and currall, as haveinge present usse of the former and Decan merchants attendinge with desire to buy the latter; which expectinge to transport over the river, we were <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-191" data-page="191" data-folio="137" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n190_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 137">[p. 137]</button> denyed boates and comanded to returne the currall againe unto the shipps, the merchants all in an uproare withstandinge your bringinge of that comoditie, as once before in our letter per the <em>Bull</em> advised you; which causinge a new question, we pretended to returne also the specie and dissolve the trade; wherein four daies beinge spent, and perceiveinge their resolutions stedfast to prohibite our bringinge of that comoditie, we passed our moneys and obtained onlie leave to howse the currall at Raneale (sealed upp by them) untill the fleete should return from Jasques, or the Prince his pleasure be further knowne concerneinge it; to whome we have written a petticion thereof and sundrie other our greivances, but have smale hope of remedie; whereby you will perceive the smale reason we have to encourage supplie of that comoditie, which if you please to enforce a trade, we do not disswade the sendinge of the former required quantities, the rather for that both at Dabull and Mesulepatnam (the ports of Decan) it will proove as vendible as in this place.' The other commodities sent are more in quantity than they desired, especially quicksilver, of which, however, an eighth had leaked out on the way. They beg that 'hereafter you wilbe pleased to observe our last advice for the quantities, and that good regard also be had unto the qualitie, packinge, and stowinge of all the comodities you send'. They were glad to hear of the safe arrival of the <em>Bull</em> in England. As regards the advisability of the earlier dispatch of ships going home, they will do their best in future, but fear that this year delays are unavoidable, owing to 'the innate accustomed villeny' of the local officials. Some of their goods for Persia have been fifteen days in the custom-house, yet 'we cannott gett them dispatched; for they will doe nothinge without bribes, which howsoever extorted is made a contynuall custome, enforced as a dutie, and yet they never contented nor wilbe made sensible of the necessitie of our hast.... Your earnestnes for encreasse of linen comodities and disesteeme of indico we also apprehend, and are verie sorrie the former (whereon our hopes depended) is not alone sufficient to answere your expectations of this trade, which we humblie entreat you will not impute to overweeninge or presumption that we plainelie declare [it?] will not be worth the followinge except constrained; for as the meare motive of our first reception was for conservation and contynuance of their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-192" data-page="192" data-folio="138" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n191_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 138">[p. 138]</button> trade into [the] Redd Sea, &amp;c., soe, the proffitt thereon ariseinge from the lynen of this countrie, they will even to the uttmost of their power hinder our investments therein ; for though this countrie be esteemed rich, we finde the common inhabitants to be verie needie, and have not meanes, nor indeede will (beinge subject to the tyranny of everie officer), to make provision of any store of comodities beforehand, contentinge themselves rather with what everie daies labour affordeth ; which also keepeth low the prizes when there is little usse, and maketh a present dearth and scarcitie uppon the least extraordinarie occasion. The place allotted to you is onlie Barroch, as aforesaid, which by good experience we finde will not yeild the eighth part of your demandes, beinge the common receptacle of Decans and all other traders both to the Red Sea and southwards. Brodra also, which is a towne of great trade and under the Kinges jurisdiction, is free unto all men, onlie the English are exempted. The meanes of remedie hath bene argued and contrasted in our last and former registers sent you, and our letters per the <em>Bull</em> gave you a tast thereof, whereto your answere beinge too spareinge for warrant, we will more particularlie touch the pointe, and referr it to your more seryous consideration. It were needlesse, we know, to shew you that the cause of our settlinge here was for feare of your shipps, and that our beinge is a burden to these, whoe have ever repined at and covertlie hindered your designes ; which nowe themselves grown prosperous, and secured by your estate in the countrie, they more apparantlie declare ; wherein by our sufferance also they are grown impudent, and will still continue to a further height of oppression, if you enforce not a feelinge of the like inconvenyencies on them. The course we propose is the stoppinge of their junks [<em>sic</em>] with the cheiffe of their people in her and meanes at [the] barr [of] Surratt, and there also to bringe that of Goga, if convenientlie to be gotten ; thereby to enforme the Kinge [that] our wrongs are insupportable, and ascertaine the Prince you will rather leave the trade then endure them ; which doubtlesse would procure whatsoever priviledges you shall desire lesse prejudiciall then the subversion of their trade, which they know cannott subsist, your people beinge hence ; and then the articles of your residence wilbe confirmed both by Kinge and Prince, and your servants (for a time) releived ; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-193" data-page="193" data-folio="139" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n192_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 139">[p. 139]</button> whome when they againe oppresse, you have the same remedie. The uttmost damadge that hereto can be aledged is the missinge of one yeares returne, which through timelie provision may also be prevented and your goods shipt aboard before theirs released. It will doubtlesse be cause of troubles and prejudice to your busines for the time, but much more to theirs, and benefitt to yours in the future. Which if you conceive necessarie to hazzard, you may please to give ample authority for itts effectinge; otherwise, if reservedlie, it will not be undertaken, the successe beinge doubtfull and of most hazzard to the factors whoe shall direct it on shoare. By this course of constraint you may also, if you please, initiate a trade at Dabull, where most of the comodities you hither send will vend, and that place affords some clothinge both for Red Sea and southwards; for England we conceive it too fine, yet were it not amisse to be experienced. Your securitie and assurance of faire dealinge must be their junks, their nature and necessitie coheireinge with their neighbours of Suratt. A further necessitie also urgeth your trade into the Redd Sea, for by the recourse of those newcome Danes and other pyratts it is greatlie to be feared some of their junks may come short, whose losse wilbe soe sensible as not onlie your returnes wilbe hindered, but your estate in the countrie questioned; of whome though we have earnestlie laboured to make them apprehend the danger, and the securitie our trade thither wilbe unto them, they are still incredible, and alledge that there are onlie three Christian nations, the Portugalls, English, and Dutch, of whome they are secured. With this feare we have endeavoured to possesse the Prince, and shall farther instance it. And if these attempts have approbation in your judgment, be pleased for the first to enorder your fleetes arrivall about the 15th or 16th September, that soe they may be here before the junks, whoe usually come the 20th or 22th ditto, or, meetinge on the waie, to accom[pany] and detaine them untill your factors come aboard, but [                    ] moderation as they be not greived nor have occasion to [jealousy?]. And if you intend to settle at Dabull also, strength of shippinge is requisite, that soe Persia may be securelie supplied and a part of your fleete emploied in the interim at Dabull, seinge the first settlinge must needes require some time; which if they refuse, their shipps that yearlie goe both <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-194" data-page="194" data-folio="140" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n193_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 140">[p. 140]</button> to Mocha and Sumatra will repaie your charge; and if they receive you, one fleete may afterwards supplie Jasques, Suratt, Dabull, and Mocha yearlie: the shipps for Mocha to returne hither, and the rest to proceede for Dabull, Sumatra, and Bantam; wherein be pleased to give the larger instructions, for that it is verie doubtfull some of us whoe now project these shall not be here to direct or see the effectinge.' The commodities provided in India for Persia amount but to 43,209 mahmūdīs 30 pice. They have not been able to send spices, &amp;c., as none have come from Bantam, and in India they are too dear. The <em>Lion</em> will be sent home on her return from Jask, whither she accompanies the fleet. She shall be dispatched as soon as possible, but they fear it will be the end of February before she can sail. <em>P.S.</em>—They enclose letters received from the Bantam Council and Captain Pring at Masulipatam, announcing the death of Sir Thomas Dale, &amp;c. (8 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, Thomas Rastell, and Giles James at Surat to Thomas Barker, &amp;c., in Persia, October</span> 29 <span class="sc">and November</span> 6, 1619 (<em>O. C.</em> 817).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-183" href="#sec-05-fn-183" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-183" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In answer to their letter from Jask dated December 10, 1618, addressed to Sir Thomas Roe. Expenses and estates of the late Francis Tipton. The rials they borrowed from the company of the <em>Expedition</em> have been debited to their account. They ought before this to have advised the prices obtained for the goods sent in that ship, as that would have been a useful guide in regard to the supply now dispatched to them. William Blundstone is to be reshipped, as ordered by Sir Thomas Roe. Some of the bales of silk received from them were opened, and the quality gave satisfaction; they were then covered with cotton and gunny, and all sent home on the <em>Royal Anne</em>. No more should be sent until orders come from home. 'You say that thirteen shahes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-184" href="#sec-05-fn-184" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-184" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and one casbegg is a royall of eight, and that two shahees maketh a ma[hmūdī]; which cannott bee, except the royall bee worth 6½ m[ahmūdīs] and one casbegg, which you knowe is seldome heere at five m[ahmūdīs].' Their request that the fleet may remain at Jask the whole season cannot <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-195" data-page="195" data-folio="141" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n194_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 141">[p. 141]</button> be granted, and they must send it back at the time appointed. No glazier has come in this fleet, and so it is to be feared that window glass is useless. Concerning William Robbins' demands. A statement of account of the old joint stock requested. Money lent by William Tracy. Details of the missing fowling-pieces. The want of weight in the ivory 'may happen through blekidge<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-185" href="#sec-05-fn-185" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-185" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> by long lying and drying in the sunne'. 'The passengers sent on the <em>Expedition</em> last yeare was at the importunitie of the Customer and some merchants heere, in hope of producing profitt by that course, but having served theire turnes could never hitherto receive ought for fraight; and therfore, seeing you also are troubled with them, we shall henceforth forbeare except one better grounds.' Would be glad to send and receive patterns and samples of goods.
