Nothing, however, could be done immediately, ‘per reason of the Governors eldest sonns <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-231" href="#sec-05-fn-231" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-231" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> death few dayes before wee came thether, and greate heavynesse in all the citty’. A petition was therefore drawn up to the Governor <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-232" href="#sec-05-fn-232" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-232" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> complaining of the detention of the caravan, of the Armenian keeping Sprage a prisoner, and of the previous robbery of indigo in his son’s ‘pregonye <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-233" href="#sec-05-fn-233" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-233" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’. ‘After four or five dayes, though the Governor were sad for his sonns death, I had admittance; who used mee kindlye, with promise of justice for all what I desired. Two dayes after I visited him againe, acquaintinge him with my busynes, who presentlye sent for the Armenian that stayed our goods and demaunded of him wherefore hee caused the goods to be detayned. Hee answered as afore specified, that was, that Swaryes had delivered to the said Bangam 20,000 ru[pees] in chenye ware etc. I tould the Governor the partye to whom hee delivered itt must answere itt. The Armenian replyed itt was our costome that what one of us did here the rest were liable to answere, and that it was true would bringe twenty wittnesses Christians. Then I said they were none but Portingalls and Armenians, which were both our enimies and knew not our costomes. The Governor at that time would here noe more, but appoynted another meetinge. The next daye after I received letters from Suratt with theire petition to the Governor, the effect beinge as I formerlye had given him, and with them the Kinge and Princes firmaens; all which after the receipt I presented to the Governor, and againe urged our busynes, and that I was goinge to the Kinge with presents and stayed onlye to know his pleasure <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-225" data-page="225" data-folio="171" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n224_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 171">[p. 171]</button> what hee would doe with our goods; and that he might perceave per those firmaens it was the Kinge and Princes pleasures wee should have all right, and that the Portingalls, beinge our enimyes, had raysed this untruth against us; who both hard and read all what I delivered unto him, and presentlye sent for the Justice of the towne to have forced the Armenian to deliver Sprage unto mee, with promise our other busynes should bee ended presentlye; but before hee came, or anye thinge elce don, hee rose without effectinge anye thinge that daye. Swaryes, notwithstandinge his absence, what with his liberalitye, or rather prodigalitye, hath to frend generallye the whole towne, both Governor, cheifes, and merchants. Soe when I perceaved and understood soe much, I expected smale justice to be don on our parts; yett to prove the uttmost, I stayed some dayes longer then I might well have don. And dayes after, the Justice of the towne sent for mee, to whom I went and found that cheetinge Armenian Suffer, havinge with him 20 or 25 wittnesses, Armenians, Moores, and Banians; where wee were all carried before the Governor, where the busynes was againe handled, the said Suffer producinge Hackim Cushalls letter, being the Princes man that went with Swaryes, and Swaryes owne letter, with a role of all the foresaid wittnesses, affirminge that Swaryes delivered into the hands of Nicholas Banggam in Bramport the foresaid some, spetified in goods, and withall that it was our costome to answer all debts, goods, and monyes one for another in these parts; to which I answered as formerlye, deniinge the knowledge of anye such goods or costome, and yf hee had delivered anye such goods to anye particuler Englishman, hee must answere for them, and that other English were not lyable to paye it nor theire goods. To which the Governor and all the Mores said that was theire justice here indeede, but the Governor said because that we were all Christians that the differance was betweene, wee should have justice accordinge to our owne costomes. I tould him there was greate difference betweene the Portingalls, Armenians, and us, both in religeon and justice, and that here were none but Armenians, and such as were never in England and knew not our costomes, and more that they were the Portingalls frends and our enimies; with that an old Armenian tould the Governor that hee had bin in Turkye, in all places where the English did trade there, and that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-226" data-page="226" data-folio="172" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n225_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 172">[p. 172]</button> theire costome was that whatsoever anye English did, the Consull in that place was lyable to paye and answere for them, and soe in all places ; and to end his false speach, said yf it were not true cutt of his head. I said itt was false, and yf there were anye such costome in England or here amoungst our nation, then I did refer myselfe to him to be disposed of atts pleasure, desiringe him to doe us the justice of this countrye, for that hee did nott understand the Christians justice in this busynes, and that these men with bribes and hope of gayne of the Portingalls did beare falce wittnesse. Notwithstandinge, the Governor demaunded of all the Armenians, as the[y] were Christians, whether that were our costomes as they had affirmed. They all answered that what one English did owe in these parts the rest with theire goods were lyable to pay itt. Uppon which the Governor, without hereinge more disputes, resolved to detayne the goods for that present, and yf in one or two months the English in Surat could send testimonye thether to the contrarye, hee would doe us right. When I saw noe hope of present release of the goods, I tould him that everye yeare our shipps did guard the Princes and merchants shipps to and from the Red Sea againe to Suratt, and therefore doubted not but wee should finde justice one waye or other. Hee answered he had noe shipps now ; yf mett with any of his, bid us take them ; yf tooke the Kinge or Princes, must give answere to them, who would stricktlye require it of us ; and what justice hee had [done ?] herein was, as hee said, for Gods sake ; and soe with this replye was forced to lett this busynes rest. Urginge him againe for Sprage, who was sent for, and att his cominge asked him what hee knew of this busynes, beinge then in Bramport, charginge him to tell the truth ; who confessed there was 13 cestas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-234" href="#sec-05-fn-234" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-234" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> or basketts of chenye dishes delivered Nicholas Banggam per Swaryes in Bramport, whereof two cestas the said Banggam caried awaye with him, and eleven hee lefte behind him in Bramport, whereof some sould, some emptye, some retorned, some broaken. Five hundred dishes Sprage confessed to have sould himselfe, which Swaryes forgave him, beinge poore, as the Armenian wittnessed ; and some Howard imbesselled. This was the account hee gave ; soe the Governor againe wished me to rest satisfied and lett the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-227" data-page="227" data-folio="173" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n226_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 173">[p. 173]</button> goods remayne in his power untill the busynes were ended ; and delivered Sprage into my hands, which I was not a little glad of, that he should beare noe witnesse against us.' Biddulph also managed to recover from the Armenian some bills he had taken from Sprage, to whom they had been entrusted by Roe to recover a debt from a fugitive in the Deccan.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-235" href="#sec-05-fn-235" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-235" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Having sent Sprage to Surat, Biddulph left John Bangham in charge of the goods and resumed his journey to Agra on April 22. The Armenian told the Governor that Roe had ordered the Surat factors to content Soares for his china ; if true, this has been the cause of much trouble and loss. Biddulph arrived at Agra May 23, and shortly after obtained a farmān for the release of the caravan and for restitution for the indigo taken from John Young's party. As this proved of no avail, a second was procured, which was more effectual, ' for uppon sight thereof all the goods were released without payment of anythinge, and the Governor alsoe gave his writinge to Giles James to the towne where the indico was forced from John Yonge that wee should have satisfaction.' It is possible, however, that Soares will give further trouble on his return from the Deccan. No doubt the Company will question Nicholas Bangham and consult Sir Thomas Roe, ' who was most acquainted with that busyness ; the Padrye [<em>see</em> p. 15 <em>n.</em>] have aften spoake to him about it.' Biddulph next solicited the Mogul's assistance in the matter of their great debt of 30,000 rupees. As the debtor himself was in His Majesty's prison, Biddulph asked that the surety might be delivered into his hands. Accordingly, ' the 25th of September last the surtye came from Lahore and hee and myselfe went before the Kinge, and after demaundinge of him whether hee were surtye, with some few other questions, caused him to be delivered into my hands and bid mee keepe him untill hee had satisfied mee, which was greate justice and as much as I cold expect from him. Soe I carried the surtye home with mee and have kept him ever since in irons ; and beinge uppon travell breeds my noe smale care and trouble leste hee should escape my hands. As yett hath paid nothinge, but at instant shewes mee meanes, as bills, etc., for 14,000 ru[pees] which divers his frends owes him neare Lahore ; soe am neare uppon dispeedinge John Willoughbye with his people to receave the monye, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-228" data-page="228" data-folio="174" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n227_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 174">[p. 174]</button> wantinge onlye Asufchans screete to the partyes, comaunding theire present payment, that I send him not uppon uncertayntyes but uppon sure grounds; makinge the surtye before Asufchan to affirme such his frends owe him soe much monye, and that hee hath assigned the English to receave itt; before whom hee dares not saye otherwise then truth.' He has sold the greater part of the fine goods at court. Loss in the measurement of the arras or hangings. 'Those of 4, 4½, and 5 ells deepe are fittest for there use, beinge they use them to put over theire heads where they sitt, and for kanottes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-236" href="#sec-05-fn-236" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-236" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, which are like pales to sett about theire tentes.' Cloth of gold and silver, velvets, satins, and gold and silver lace will sell well; but 'there is noe certayntye of the request of anye fine goods in the court, for they are soe variable they change theire mindes in a moment and desire yearlye new and strange things, they know not what themselves; soe it is not easye to fitt their humours'. 'Greate pearles from twenty to forty carratts, orientall and faire, are the presentest money of any commoditye and most in request at present.' Armour, swords, or pictures should be sent only for presents. Another caravan dispatched from Agra to Surat on October 3. 'The 7th of the same month the Kinge departed Agra uppon his intended jornye for Cashmere, which is 450 courses from Agra;' and on the 10th November Biddulph and Willoughby, with Hawkridge in attendance, followed him. He has done his best with the Prince and others to procure liberty to trade in the Red Sea, but 'notwithstandinge what I could doe, the Governour and all the merchants in Suratt petitio[n]ing the Prince not to graunt us that trade, for yf hee did they were all undon and the cittye begered, havinge noe other place to trade unto but the Red Sea, which they were contented with, leavinge the southwards and other places for the English, the Prince and all generallye tendringe theire owne peoples goods and complaynts before our shutes and benifitts, and not beleevinge wee durst or would use force against them att sea, havinge soe manye English in divers places in this countrye and such quantityes of goods as yearelye wer brought in and remayned still in these parts, the cheife men tellinge of itt, soe that they thinke themselves safe with these pawnes and their owne pride, which blinds them soe that they wil beleeve nothinge untill <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-229" data-page="229" data-folio="175" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n228_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 175">[p. 175]</button> itt falls uppon theire heads; all which did cause an absolut denyall to that trade, the Prince wishinge mee to trouble him noe more with that busynes; which answere I advised to Surat, with my opinion to make staye of theire shipp then expected with the <em>Lyon</em> from the Red Sea, yf itt were but for a few dayes, that the Kinge and Prince might take notice that wee both could and would force that trade, yf per faire meanes wee could not procure itt. But itt seemed per theire answere to that poynt they and Amadavas cold not agree to performe itt, unlesse expresse order from Your Worshipps; which yf had soe fallen out that they had agreed with my opinion, they had bin prevented per theire shipps not retorne from the Red Sea this yeare, per reason of theire late departure the roade and the munsones tur[n]inge were forced to staye till the next yeare. I onlye gave my opinion to staye hir for a few dayes, that the Governor and merchants might have written the Prince thereof, and afterwards released (untill expresse order from Your Worshipps), which would have bin little hindrance to our busynes but rather a furtherance; but I hope in God all was for the best, and must saye they worke surlye that have warrant for theire proceedings. Since which I received letters uppon travell from Surat after the arivall of the fleete, with theire petitions to the Prince and Asufchan, wherein againe they urged the Mocha trade, and that the Governor and cheifes of Suratt would not suffer them to bringe theire corrall to Suratt to sell (which was a new force never spoaken of before), neither would suffer them to have a house suffitient for themselves and goods. Uppon receipte of theire petitions and letter of advise of the wrongs offred them, I first acquanted Asufchan and the Princes cheife nobleman<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-237" href="#sec-05-fn-237" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-237" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> with the abuses offred us, and that itt was not accordinge to divers firmaens given our nation per the Kinge and Prince for our free trade and good usage in all places in the Kinges dominions. Theire answere was the Prince was kinge in Suratt and might use his pleasure; but they would assist us in our busynes to him what they could, but to complayn to the Kinge his father of him would but incense him the more against us, beinge continewallye att his fathers elbow, who would not discontent his sonn to pleasure us; wishinge us to have a little patience, for it was liklye hee should not longe continew <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-230" data-page="230" data-folio="176" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n229_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 176">[p. 176]</button> in that place, which God graunt prove true. Few dayes after this conference, I went to the Prince and delivered him theire petition from Suratt, the Governor and cheifes of Suratt havinge the same time petitioned the Prince to contradict what the English desired for the Red Sea trade; and for the corrall, it was the cheife commoditye came from that place. And after perusall and some questions, he absolutlye tould mee wee should not trade to the Red Sea, havinge given mee in Agra answere suffitient, nor bringe anye corrall into these partes to sell; and yf could not be contented to have free trade for all but Mocha, wee might goe out of the countrye yf wee would, for must not begger his people for us; but yf would have his firmaen for a house and free trade in all other places with good usage, wee should have one. I tould him yf had not free trade to buy and sell in all places, as costome of merchants were, wee needed not neither house nor firmaen. Att that instant was present one Jemoll Chan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-238" href="#sec-05-fn-238" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-238" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, who was last yeare Governour of Surat, who did affirme before the Prince that the last yeare, before Sir Thomas Roes departure, he promised him and some of the cheife merchants of Suratt that yf they would suffer him to lade those goods alreadye bought for the Redd Sea, beinge nott fitt for owne countrye, hee would passe his word wee should send noe more to the Red Sea, and uppon those promises they lett them passe the last yeare. I tould him there was noe such matter promised, for myselfe was there then present. Soe bid us trouble him noe more with that busynes. I doe well remember Sir Thomas Roe had much to doe the last yeare in Suratt to shipp the goods for Mocha, which the merchaunts graunted at last but absolutlye tould him wee shold send noe more, and Sir Thomas Roe answered yf they did petition to the Prince they should be answered. This was all the remedye was sought per him before his departure. Not lettinge the busynes soe fall, seeinge the necessitye, I caused a short petition to be drawne to the Prince for the things needfull, and not longe after (beinge as soone as possiblye I could, considringe it was uppon travell) I againe presented itt unto him; which after perusall seemed verye much displeased, wished mee to forbeare my shute. Soe Asufchan speakinge in our behalfes, hee graunted his firmaen for sale of our corrall in Suratt or elcewhere for this yeare, and for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-231" data-page="231" data-folio="177" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n230_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 177">[p. 177]</button> our old house in Surat, which they were turned out of, or another to theire contents; with comaund of present dispatch of our goods out of the costome house, both inwards and outwards, and to use our people kindlye. For the Mocha trade, hee would not here of itt. This firmaen I was att present forced to take, not knowinge anye remidye; which few dayes past was effected. Soe Your Worshipps may perceave what trouble the Prince doth yearlye give us, which there is smale hopes will ever be otherwise soe longe as hee is in such favour with his father and hath Surat in his hands. For the Mocha trade and free sale for corrall, the former will never be graunted but with much difficultye, and for the other noe certayntye thereof. Soe that in my opinionn you must either resolve to lett fall the Red Sea trade or beare the hazard of your goods and merchants in this countrye yf you order the seazure and takeinge of theire shipp and goods, which questionlesse wilbee a greate adventure of youre goods and trade, and absolutlye will that yeare, whensoever it is putt in pracktise, hinder the sendinge of a shipp for England and goods to the southwards. These are the best can be expected; soe must referr the effectinge to Your Worshipps commaunds, which for my owne parte I shalbee ever readye to obaye soe theire maye be apparance itt maye prove in all liklyehood for your benefitts, and otherwise I am perswaded none in these parts will condesend thereunto.’ Biddulph advises that no more coral be sent till matters are on a better footing. He is surprised at the small number of presents sent this year, as little can be done at court without them. Pay of Thomas Hawkridge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘It is uncertayne att present where the Kinge will settle, but is generally reported hee will make a mounthes staye 30 courses beyond Lahore, and afterwards proceede on his progresse for Cashmere, which is 150 courses farther, and there staye two or three mounthes and afterwards retorne for Lahore. But how longe hee will remayne there, or whither from thence hee will goe, is not knowne. His owne resolutions are uncertayne, beinge lead per a woman; therefore must referr the truth to time.’ Biddulph has ordered any further goods for court to be kept at Agra till it is known where the King will settle, as the camp is full of thieves. He hopes the Company will consider his ‘paynes and travell’. (<em>Endorsed</em> ‘Receaved the 27 September, 1620’. 8½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-05-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The quotations are in many cases from the abstract, as the copy of the letter is in a very bad condition.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps the <em>asām</em>, or Indian prayer-rug, is here intended. It is usually striped blue and white.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">From the <em>Court Minutes</em> of Sept. 22, 1624, it appears that ‘nicanees’ were striped calicoes of a somewhat higher price than usual. Valentyn, in his list of Surat piece-goods (bk. 4, pt. 2, p. 146), gives ‘niquanias’, 14¼ ells long and 1⅛ broad.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Watchets were blue-coloured woollens made at the Somersetshire town of that name. Here the term is applied to the distinctive colour of these stuffs.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>bābāghūrī</em>, the white agate of Cambay, so called from the patron saint of the district in which the mines are situated.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. v. p. 137.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ibid., p. 121.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ibid., p. 110.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the incident see Dale's letter to the Company (<em>Hague Transcripts</em> at I. O., ser. i, vol. iii); another letter from him entered as No. 609 in the <em>Calendar of State Papers</em>, <em>E. Indies</em>, 1617–21, and quoted in part in the preface; John Pery's letters (<em>O. C.</em> 759); Arnold Brown's account of the voyage in <em>Purchas</em> (vol. ii. p. 1850); a letter from Nicolas Trigautius, also in <em>Purchas</em> (vol. ii. p. 1693); Faria y Sousa's <em>Asia Portuguesa</em> (vol. iii. pt. iii. ch. 15); and a letter from the Portuguese Viceroy in the <em>Lisbon Transcripts</em> at the I. O. (<em>Documentos Remettidos</em>, vol. xi). There is some doubt as to the amount extorted from the carrack; Dale says 80,000 rials, but the Portuguese agree with the text in making it 90,000. It may be mentioned in passing that the second letter from Dale alluded to above is wrongly dated in the <em>Calendar</em> as March 1, whereas it should be March 15; that it appears <span class="folio-turn">[p. 54]</span> to be rather a draft or signed copy than a real letter; and that it was evidently addressed, not to the Company, but to a relative, probably Sir William Throgmorton (Dale's brother-in-law). A letter from Dale to Throgmorton of the same date and dealing with the same subjects is described in the seventh report of the Hist. MSS. Commission as being in Mr. Molyneux's collection.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ādil Shāh, King of Bījāpur.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wine from Alicante.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As pointed out in <em>Hobson-Jobson</em> (s. v. ‘Bulgar’), this term has nothing to do with modern Bulgaria. It was used to denote ‘Russia leather’ (cp. the ‘Muscovy hides’ of p. 21), from the fact that the region of manufacture and export was originally Bolghār, on the Volga.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The petty kingdom of the Kelādi chiefs of Ikkeri, now a town of small importance in the Shimoga district of Mysore. The port of Bhatkal, twenty-five miles south of Honāvar, had been added to their territory some twenty years previous to this date. The outcome of these negotiations will be found in Hoare’s letter on a later page.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">It appears from the abstract that these were to be brought to the Company’s account —a rule ever after maintained.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Zeila.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Berbera.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Dahlak, on an island of the same name, about thirty miles east of Massowah. It is said to be the Orina of the Romans. Cp. <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. i. p. 192, where ‘Dialeque’ is said to be twenty-five days from ‘Dombia’. Massowah would of course be the actual point of departure.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Suakin.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is still a province of Dembea in Abyssinia, to the north of Lake Tsana, but the chief town is now called Gondar.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Zambesi (the Zuama of Barbosa, Cuama of Leo Africanus and Linschoten, Couesme of Pyrard de Laval).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Monomotapa,’ which Burton explains as ‘Lord of M’tapa’ (M’wene M’tapa), a native kingdom on the Zambesi.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not extant.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Turban-cloths.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khān-khānān ('lord of lords'), the title of Mīrzā Abdul Rahīm, one of the leading nobles of the time. He was the son of Akbar's celebrated general, Bairām Khān, and <span class="folio-turn">[p. 60]</span> first distinguished himself by crushing Muzaffar Shāh's attempt to recover the throne of Gujarāt. He held in turn the governments of Jaunpur, Multān, and Sind, and was at this time in charge of the operations against the Deccan princes. Withington (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 483) refers to him as 'Chon Chin Naw, the greatest of the Mogoll Nobilitie'. The son here referred to was Shāhnawāz Khān, often styled i-Jahāngīrī, to distinguish him from another person of the same name.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The unit by which indigo was generally bought. It is often termed by the factors a bundle (or fardle). The greater churl was a little over five maunds in weight; the smaller, about four.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This may mean either the Amīr Mīrān, or a title—<em>mīr-mīrān</em>, 'chief of chiefs.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">It appears from the abstract that Kerridge added a further postscript begging for a continuance of the £50 per annum allowed him by Keeling, for 'adventures in the joint stockes', and for a gratuity, alleging that he had fully intended to return to England this year, but remained at Roe's special request.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In most cases expenses of carriage, &amp;c., seem to have added to the actual price paid.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On the much disputed question of the origin of ‘gingham’ see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em> (2nd ed.), p. 375.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>kori</em>, a score.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">In some notes derived from Nicholas Ufflet (<em>Fact. Rec. Miscell.</em>, vol. xxv) ‘resses’ are described as ‘fine dimatees from two to three ropees per peece, yarde and haulfe broad’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">This may be a corrupt form of <em>asmānī</em>, ‘sky-blue,’ i. e. watchet-colour (see p. 51).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 51 <em>n.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly there is some connexion with <em>ihrām</em>, the cotton dress assumed by the pilgrim on entering Mecca. In a later letter (Oct. 29, 1619) the term is used as synonymous with <em>dhotīs</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">From Dholka.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A general term for precious stones and gems (Port. <em>pedraria</em>). Roe speaks of ‘pearle and other <em>pedreria</em>’ (<em>O. C.</em> 610).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Oxford Eng. Dict.</em> defines ‘brawl’ as ‘a blue and white striped cloth manufactured in India’. Possibly the name may be derived from the Hind. <em>bīral</em>, ‘open in texture, fine.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ufflet (see p. 61 <em>n</em>) describes ‘tapsels’ as ‘a stripe stuff with silk and cotton, from seven rupees to twelve.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the same as ‘madafunum’, described in <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. i. p. 74, as ‘chequered and somewhat fine’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps Hind. <em>korā</em>, ‘plain, unbleached.’ On <em>lungīs</em> (waist-cloths) see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd ed., p. 519.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jāmbusar, in Broach district, was formerly a centre of indigo export.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Crude lac (Hindi <em>ras</em>, ‘crude’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a copy in <em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2122 (<em>f.</em> 27).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Consisting of dogs, spangles, and a brass cannon nine feet long, with a bore of two feet, which had been sent in response to the request made by the King of Achin in a letter <span class="folio-turn">[p. 65]</span> to King James brought home by Keeling in May, 1617. The Company agreed with Thomas Pitt to make the gun at a cost of about 200<em>l.</em>; but it turned out a failure, and the King, finding it ‘soe thin of substance as not to be shott of, he estemed it nothing worth, nor woulde except of it’. He seems, however, to have retained it. Dampier, when at Achin in 1690, heard that there were brass guns at the palace which had been presented by King James; and Mr. Dudley Hervey, C.M.G., tells me that when he was there in 1870 he saw lying just inside the gates of the palace a gun bearing the name of King James, the date 1617, and an inscription stating that it had been made by William and Richard Pitt. There can be little doubt that this was the identical piece sent out by the Company in 1618.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>The Embassy</em>, p. 518 <em>n.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">David Middleton sailed from Bantam for England in the <em>Samaritan</em>, April 3, 1615. A rumour from Dutch sources affirmed that she had been wrecked on Madagascar, that her crew had succeeded in getting on shore with the goods, and that one of them had been seen by the Dutch at the port of ‘Casseen’ (Kishin) in Southern Arabia (<em>Letters Received</em>, vol. v. p. 159, vol. vi. p. 50); and some years later (see Jeffries’ letter of Nov. 18, 1621) came a report that she had been lost at Mauritius. Evidently the Company had instructed Bonner to make inquiries for the castaways.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Linschoten (following Marco Polo) declares that Madagascar has ‘great store of woodes of redde sandale, which are there little esteemed for the great abundance’. It was probably this passage that prompted Bonner’s search.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Destroyed by Joseph’s fleet in 1616 (see <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. v. p. 142, &amp;c.).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘A kind of sickle, formerly applied to the yardarms for cutting the rigging of a vessel running on board’ (Smyth’s <em>Sailor’s Word Book</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Case-shot filled with rough pieces of iron and used to damage an enemy’s rigging and sails.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">A factor in Capt. Bonner’s fleet. His letter, written about May, 1620, has been printed in Purchas’s first volume (p. 656), though with some mistakes. We have here extracted the portion relating to the fleet’s voyage down the western coast of India, and its attempts to trade at Dābhol, Bhatkal, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The 21st, according to Purchas’s version.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ponānī, in lat. 10° 47′, at the mouth of a river of the same name.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Interpreter (Malay <em>jurubahāsa</em>, ‘master of speech’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Mandīl</em>, a turban-cloth woven with silk and gold thread.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A puzzling term, possibly connected with Hind. <em>pālān</em>, ‘a pack-saddle or dorser.’ The Agra factors, under date of Dec. 15, 1619, call them ‘canesters’, meaning probably baskets (see <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 115).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A very early example of English use of the well-known antithesis of <em>kachchā</em> (raw, temporary, slight) and <em>pakkā</em> (ripe, permanent, solid).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>malmal</em>, muslin.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>tīrandāz</em>, an archer.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>tānt</em>, thread, fibre, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>dēg</em>, a large cooking-pot.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>sarpūsh</em>, a cover.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>mashak</em>, a leathern water-bag.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">8.</a> <span class="fn-body">Marches or stages (Arabic <em>manzil</em>, ‘alighting’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">9.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Mukaddam</em>, a head-man or leader.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">10.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Maqām</em>, a halt; cp. Finch (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 424), ‘which made us make <em>mukom</em>’ (marg. note ‘<em>mukom</em>, stay for refreshing’).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">11.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Bandūqdār</em>, a musketeer. Bangham had in the first instance written ‘shott’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">12.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dhāita, on the road from Burhānpur to Surat, near the junction of the Surpīnī River with the Tāptī. Here Partab Shāh, the chief of Bāglān, levied transit dues on all caravans, though Roe had recently obtained a <em>farmān</em> from him promising exemption for English goods (<em>Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 131).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Roe had stipulated (see p. 38) that a suitable dwelling should be found for him; and Terry (<em>Voyage to India</em>, p. 201), after describing the pleasant abodes of the wealthier natives, with their gardens, tanks, &amp;c., adds: 'in such a garden-house, with all those accommodations about it, my Lord Ambassadour lay with his company at Surat the last three moneths before he left East India.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The factors did not dare to make this ridiculous charge against Roe in their letters to the Company; but as copies of all correspondence were sent home, they no doubt counted upon its coming to their masters' notice in this indirect fashion.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Turban-cloths.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The term ‘gad’ was applied to both bars and ingots.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>nishādur</em>, ‘sal-ammoniac.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bījāpur. The person alluded to is probably the 'Dutchman, great with the King' mentioned in <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. v. pp. 174, 323.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This letter, sent by a native junk to Mokha for conveyance overland, is partly written in cipher (explained by the factors as being 'your carracters sent forth on Captain Bests voyage'), but the disguise is very thin. A longhand copy will be found in <em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2122.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A well-known term for the Hindu traders of Gujarāt.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jahāngīr, on leaving Ahmadābād in Sept. 1618, proceeded to Agra.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mailed, i. e. sheathed in some defensive material. Our modern ‘mail’, applied to the transmission of packets by the post, is an interesting instance of transference of meaning.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hindus. This word and ‘Gentoo’ are corruptions of the Port. <em>Gentio</em> (see <em>Hobson</em>-<em>Jobson</em>, 2nd ed., p. 367).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Fatehpur Sīkrī, twenty-three miles from Agra.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Hobson-Jobson</em> for <em>kos</em>, the usual measure of distance in India. It varies widely in different parts, but may be roughly taken at about two miles.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">In Kerridge’s handwriting.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">This alarm was not without some justification. King Philip wrote to the Viceroy at Goa in Feb., 1618, urging that a squadron of galleons and frigates should be sent to occupy the port of Swally before the arrival of the English fleet; but the Viceroy replied a year later that he could not carry out the scheme for want of men, money, and munitions of war. In March, 1620, the King again urged the project, but the same answer was returned. Two years later he suggested that two old ships laden with stones should be sunk at the entrance of the port in order to block it; to this the Viceroy objected that such a course would probably anger the Great Mogul, and would merely drive the English to neighbouring ports. See the <em>Lisbon Transcripts</em> at I. O., <em>Documentos Remettidos</em>, vol. v.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Coffee (Arab. <em>qahwa</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Man-i-shāhī</em> (royal maund).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>shāhī</em> was worth about fourpence (<em>Letters Received</em>, vol. iii. pp. 176, 326).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Balūchīs, employed as guards.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers.-Hind. <em>rāhdārī</em>, or charge for guarding the road.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Seed-lac (<em>lākh dānā</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shell-lac (<em>chapra lākh</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">It is evident from this and other passages that the factors used 'chintes' as a plural. Our 'chintzes' is therefore a reduplication. Cf. the remarks in the <em>Oxford Eng. Dict.</em>, s. v.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>hundī</em>, a bill of exchange; hence rupees remitted in that manner.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>arthiyā</em>, a broker or agent.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Villages (Port. <em>aldea</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Hāq Pārak. ‘The Pāraks were an eminent family of <em>banyas</em> in Surat and Diu. They are often mentioned in the Company’s correspondence with Surat’ (<em>Hedges’ Diary</em>, vol. ii. p. 151). On July 20, 1683, the Court voted to ‘Bingee Parack, the Companies Banyan at Suratt’, a gold medal and chain of the value of 150<em>l.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bhadwar. Finch (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 424) places it 10 <em>kos</em> east of Dhāita and 7 <em>kos</em> west of Nandurbār; he describes it as 'a filthy towne and full of theeves'. He also says that 'this towne is the last of note in Pectopshaws [Partab Shāh; see p. 74 <em>n.</em>] land', which accounts for the levying of tolls there.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>lōhā</em>, 'iron,' and <em>botā</em>, 'a lump or piece.' The ironworker of Gujarāt is still called a <em>lōhār</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The 'henselle' of p. 76 and 'henslaus' of p. 94. The derivation is not known.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Francisco Soares, at whose instigation the English caravan had been arrested, was a Portuguese merchant who appears to have been engaged in bringing jewels to the court from Goa and other places. Roe calls him ‘the prodigal Portugall’, and mentions that he was about to marry Mrs. Towerson’s aunt (<em>Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 141).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is evidently the copyist’s mistake for ‘miradall’, i. e. <em>Mīr-adl</em>, ‘Chief Justice.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nosārī or Nausārī, in Baroda territory, about twenty miles south of Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>jāgīr</em>, an assignment of land and the income derived therefrom.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On the road from Burhānpur to Surat, about sixty miles from the former city.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Court (Pers. <em>darbār</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Mulher, near Jaitāpur, in Bāglān, is intended. Finch in 1610 speaks of ‘Muliere, where the mamudees are coyned’ (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 424).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>khazāna</em>, ‘treasure.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>kitāba</em>, 'a written order.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These were the guns which had been saved from the wreck of the <em>Middelburg</em>. They were sold to the native authorities, who mounted them on the walls of the castle; see Van Twist's <em>Generale Beschrijvinge van Indien</em>, 1648, p. 10.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Following native custom, the factors at Surat and elsewhere used oxen to draw their coaches.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These names of piece-goods should be compared with those on p. 62, and with the list in <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd ed., p. 705.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mahr. <em>tasū</em>, the twenty-fourth part of a covid.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dhārangāon, in Khāndesh district, thirty-five miles north-east of Dhūliā.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">A ‘chokaree’, ‘chuckree,’ or ‘chockrie’ (Hind. <em>chakar</em>, ‘a roll’) contained four pieces; see <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. iv. p. 239, vol. vi. p. 236.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malay <em>ikat</em>, ‘to bind,’ and <em>pingang</em>, ‘the waist.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Canekeen’ is said to be from the Mahrattī <em>khandakī</em> (Sanskrit <em>khaṇḍaka</em>, ‘a piece or fragment’) through the Port. <em>canequim</em>; and in ‘trycandee’ we may perhaps trace the Sanskrit and Mahrattī <em>trikānda</em>, meaning ‘three sections’. From this passage and a letter to Persia given below (Oct. 29, 1619), it appears that the former was made from <em>bāftas</em> and the latter from <em>dhotī</em>s, and that their names were due to ‘theire shortnes and dying’ respectively.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>inām</em>, 'a gift.' The word is used in Western India as equivalent to <em>bakhshīsh</em> (<em>see Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd ed., p. 433).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Khānkhānān.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Submit a petition (Hind. <em>arzī</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>mīrdaha</em>, ‘a chief of ten;’ here probably the leaders of the small escort of Balūchīs.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Hind. <em>pāra</em>, ‘a strap.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Pātan, on the Saraswatī, in Baroda territory.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Robert Freeman was appointed a factor in Oct., 1617, and went out in Bonner's fleet. Roe selected him for employment at Agra, but he did not live to take up the post.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 31 <em>n.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">A customs or toll-station (Hind. <em>chaukī</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Caravanserai (Pers. <em>karwānsarāī</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is probably the same as Van Twist’s ‘goutom’, which he describes as a silk cloth with coloured stripes (<em>Generale Beschrijvinge van Indien</em>, p. 63).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>kahār</em>, the name of a caste generally employed to carry palankins and articles of a particularly breakable nature. Cf. Peter Mundy's account of them (Brit. Mus. <em>Harl. MS.</em> 2286), written about ten years later than the text:—‘A Cahare is a fellowe that on a peece of bamboo or greate cane which lyes on his shoulder will carrie at either end thereof half a quintall, with which hee will travell 25 or 30 mile a daie, for hee goes a kinde of an easie leasinge pace, or as it were gentlie runninge, the bamboo yeildinge and bendinge at everie stepp, soe that theie carrie more steddie then any other kinde of invention that I knowe. Theie are most commonlie imployed for carryinge of chinae, christall or anie curious brittle ware; alsoe of meate and drinke or anie liquid thinge. Anie greate man, when hee travells, hath manie of these Cahares alonge with him for the purposes aforesaid.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>mazūr</em>, a corruption of <em>mazdūr</em>, ‘a carrier or porter.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>momjāma</em>, ‘wax-cloth.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>tarī</em>, ‘a boat.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rājāpur, in Ratnāgiri district.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A present to a superior (Pers. <em>pīshkash</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>nishān</em>, ‘a signet or emblem;’ hence ‘a signed order’, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>farmān-navīs</em>, an official whose duty it was to draw up farmāns and similar documents.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>arz-dāsht</em>, a written petition or memorial.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sent open to Surat, where Rastell added his signature and dispatched the letter to James.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-137" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A slip for ‘Āsaf Khān’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sironj, in Tonk State, Rājpūtāna, on the Burhānpur-Agra road.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khurram and (perhaps) Parwīz.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-140" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-140" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This date has been wrongly read as July 13 in the <em>Cal. of State Papers: E. Indies</em>, 1617–21.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-141" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-141" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nīlapallī, on the Coringa branch of the Godāvarī. Pring’s fleet from Bantam had been driven by contrary winds to the north-east of Masulipatam and had been forced to anchor in the Bay of Coringa. Ball went in the ship’s boat up the creek to Nīlapallī, and thence dispatched letters to Masulipatam. Methwold at once hastened to the fleet and arranged for a supply of provisions for their use. He and Ball then proceeded to Masulipatam, arriving July 19. Pring’s ships did not reach that place till Sept. 7.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-142" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-142" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Engano, an island off the south-west coast of Sumatra, about 125 miles from the Straits of Sunda. The <em>Sun</em>, of Sir Thomas Dale’s fleet, was wrecked there on Nov. 16, 1618.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-143" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-143" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Master of the <em>Bear</em>. See <em>The Embassy</em> (p. 519) for his meeting with Roe at the Cape, and the letter sent by the ambassador to Jourdain in consequence of Barwick’s information that the two Companies were on the point of reconciliation.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-144" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-144" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Queen Anne died March 2, 1619. Ralegh was executed Oct. 29, 1618.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-145" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-145" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For an interesting note on the <em>Ahadīs</em> see the new edition of <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, p. 408. They were a class of ‘gentlemen-troopers’ in attendance on the sovereign or (as in this case) his sons, and were largely used as messengers or for special missions. Hawkins estimates their number at 5,000; to which Purchas adds that ‘M[r. W.] Clarke (which divers yeeres served the Mogol in his warres and was one of these haddies) saith 30,000’ (<em>Pilgrimage</em>, p. 521).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-146" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-146" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There seems to be here a confusion between <em>tasar</em> silk and the grass-cloth (<em>herba</em>) of Bengal.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-147" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-147" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ankleswar, six miles south of Broach. They are on opposite sides of the Narbadā, and the tolls were doubtless exacted, as usual, on crossing the river.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-148" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-148" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Ganj-āwar</em>, ‘Treasure-bearer.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-149" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-149" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shehr, a port on the south-west coast of Arabia.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-150" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-150" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Newly-coined (from <em>korā</em>, ‘fresh,’ and <em>sikka</em>, ‘a coining die’). New coins passed at a higher rate than old ones.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-151" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-151" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Dutch.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Capital.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the 'Captain of the Castle'.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>kānūn-gū</em>, 'an expounder of the law.' In Upper India the term is applied to a revenue officer.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-155" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-155" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A term applied to various kinds of peas or beans used for food on board ship.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-156" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-156" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Chana</em>, or chick-pea.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-157" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-157" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently some kind of <em>dāl</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-158" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-158" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Machiavellian.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-159" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-159" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>dabbah</em>, a tub made of hides to hold <em>ghee</em> or butter.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-160" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-160" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In the fleet commanded by John Bickley (or Bickell), consisting of the <em>Charles</em>, <em>Ruby</em>, and <em>Diamond</em>. The <em>Palsgrave</em>, <em>Elizabeth</em>, and <em>Hope</em>, bound for Bantam, were in company as far as the Cape.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-161" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-161" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was the fleet dispatched in Nov., 1618, by the newly-founded Danish East India Company (see introduction). It consisted of five vessels (not six, as stated above; cf. p. 136), and was commanded by Ove Gjedde.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-162" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-162" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mainly nautical notes and directions.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-163" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-163" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Representatives of the States-General. For these negotiations see the preface to the <em>Calendar of State Papers, E. Indies, 1617–21.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-164" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-164" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>tuhfa</em>, 'a present.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-165" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-165" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">This word (written later 'musserad') is probably the Pers. <em>masarrat</em>, 'a cause of joy,' applied to the Prince's written or spoken approval of his officers' actions. The sense here seems to be that the latter, in their eagerness to earn the Prince's commendations, looked very sharply after all curiosities from England, of which by agreement he was to have first choice.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-166" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-166" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. Shāh Jahān (the Prince’s title).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-167" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-167" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 173, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-168" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-168" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably some junior factors or masters’ mates left on board the fleet.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-169" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-169" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Variao, on the right bank of the Tāptī, about three miles north of Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-170" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-170" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">From subsequent references it appears that these were native carriers.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-171" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-171" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rānder, on the right bank of the Tāptī, two miles above Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-172" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-172" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sealed (Hind. <em>chhâp</em>, 'a seal-impression').</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-173" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-173" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Olpād, about twelve miles north-west of Surat. Cf. <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. iv. p. 329.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-174" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-174" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The copy is dated the 23rd, but a subsequent reference, and its position in the book, show that this is a slip.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-175" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-175" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably John Curtis, master’s mate of the <em>Lion</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-176" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-176" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Master of the <em>Lion</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-177" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-177" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Himmat Khān, Governor of Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-178" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-178" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīrzā Muhammad Yār, the Governor's brother.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-179" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-179" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Lion</em> had come out in Bonner's fleet, and was therefore in a quasi-independent position.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-180" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-180" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a diet-table of a few years later see <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. v. p. 163.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-181" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-181" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a damaged copy in <em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2122, f. 169.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-182" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-182" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'October' is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-183" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-183" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a damaged copy in <em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2122, <em>f.</em> 162.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-184" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-184" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 83 <em>n.</em> Ten <em>cosbegs</em> went to the <em>shāhī</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-185" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-185" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This form is not given in the <em>Oxford Eng.
