Dict.</em>, though under 'bleak' (a form of 'bleach') we find an apt quotation from Cotgrave's <em>Dict.</em> (1611): '<em>haler</em>, to <em>bleak</em> or make swart a thing by displaying it in a hot sunne.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-186" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-186" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Sanskrit name for aloes-wood was <em>agaru</em> or <em>aguru</em>, whence the Malayālam <em>akil</em> and the Port. <em>aguila</em>. This last was corrupted into <em>aquila</em>, and from the confusion arose the French <em>bois d'aigle</em> and Eng. <em>eagle-wood</em>. (See <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, s. v.)</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-187" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-187" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not saffron but turmeric (Port. <em>açafrão da terra</em>). ‘Zawchobe’ is the Persian name, <em>zard-chūbah</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-188" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-188" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was the fleet dispatched from Portugal in the spring of 1619 under Ruy Freire de Andrade.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-189" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-189" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Rev. Matthew Cardro.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-190" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-190" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The journal goes on to narrate the sailing of the fleet for Sumatra and Java on March 16, 1620, visiting Dābhol on the way.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-191" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-191" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Merza Jam̃ Kullee Beage' [Mīrzā Jam Qulī Beg]. The letter is undated, but evidently belongs to about November 10–15.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-192" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-192" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Undated.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-193" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-193" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Signature (Ital. <em>firma</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-194" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-194" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>pregão</em>, 'a proclamation.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-195" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-195" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cutchcrry (Hind. <em>kachahrī</em>), the place where public business was transacted.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-196" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-196" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Footmen, messengers, or (as here) servants (Port. <em>peão</em>, 'a footman').</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-197" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-197" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">Courier or messenger (Arabic <em>qāsid</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-198" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-198" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Bachcha betī-chod</em>—a piece of abuse too gross for translation.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-199" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-199" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not extant. The other two are given below.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-200" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-200" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Undated.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-201" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-201" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>gharī</em>, a clepsydra or water-clock, and hence the interval of time (about 24 minutes) indicated by the clock. 'They distinguish their time in a much different manner from us, dividing the day into four, and the night into as many parts, which they call Pores [Hind. <em>pahr</em>]; which again they subdivide each of them into eight parts, which they call Grees, measured, according to the ancient custom, by water dropping out of one vessel into another' (Terry's <em>Voyage to East-India</em>, ed. 1777, p. 230).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-202" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-202" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Undated.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-203" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-203" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The reference is of course to Capt. Best's negotiations with the native authorities for the establishment of a factory.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-204" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-204" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Qulich Khān, Sūbadār of Gujarāt, on whom see a note in Blochmann's <em>Āīn-i-Akbarī</em>, vol. i. p. 34. Hawkins mentions ‘Kelich Chan’ as a mansabdār of 9,000 horse.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-205" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-205" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shaikh Yūsuf.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-206" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-206" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Dīwān</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-207" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-207" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps Mīrzā Faiz-ullah.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-208" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-208" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khwāja Hasan Alī.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-209" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-209" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Shāh-bandar</em>, ‘harbour-master.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-210" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-210" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Sag-māda bachcha</em>, ‘son of a bitch.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-211" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-211" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The pagoda was worth at this time about 7<em>s.</em> 6<em>d.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-212" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-212" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pring was equally emphatic in his condemnation of the conduct of ‘this irregular and almost incorrigible scumme of rascals whome the land hath ejected for theyr wicked lives and ungodly behaviour. Our misery is that wee often see the proverbe fulfilled, which is: Yf they bee good for nothing, send them to the East Indies’ (<em>O. C.</em> 784).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-213" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-213" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Conde de Redondo (see p. 31) died Nov. 10, 1619 (N.S.), and Fernão de Albuquerque took up the reins of administration as Governor, pending the appointment of a Viceroy from home.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-214" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-214" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The island in the Sunda Straits now called Sebesi, between the islands of Krakatau and Seboekoe.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-215" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-215" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A headland and road on the southern side of Sumatra, not far from the entrance to the Straits of Sunda (see <em>Purchas his Pilgrimes</em>, vol. i. p. 451). Pring's account of the voyage will be found at p. 637 of that volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-216" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-216" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 110. For the consultation at which this disposal of the ships was agreed upon, see <em>O. C.</em> 801.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-217" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-217" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Cf. The Embassy of Sir T. Roe</em>, p. 518.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-218" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-218" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Or, as we now say, ‘against the grain.’ Cf. <em>Merry Wives of Windsor</em>, ii. 3 : ‘If you should fight, you go against the hair of your professions.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-219" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-219" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The rial seems to be taken at 4<em>s.</em> 6<em>d.</em> and the pagoda at 7<em>s.</em> 6<em>d.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-220" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-220" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nothing is known of him or his mission from Roe, beyond what is stated in these documents.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-221" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-221" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the broadcloths purchased for the use of the Prince. On the many meanings of ‘sircar’ (<em>sarkār</em>) see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, s. v. Here and elsewhere in these extracts it is used almost in the sense of the ‘privy purse’ of modern times.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-222" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-222" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘That kind of duttie is as thin as boulter [cloth used for sifting meal]; twice so much cotton wooll were not inough’ (<em>Marginal note made at home</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-223" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-223" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">We hear much of these in later letters from Patna. The name may possibly be connected with Hind. <em>amṛita</em>, ‘ambrosia.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-224" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-224" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Dastūrī</em> to the Prince’s officers, no doubt.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-225" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-225" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘ Venis ’ in margin (? Venice).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-226" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-226" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pulicat. The visit of the <em>Zeeland</em> to Masulipatam is mentioned in a letter from the Dutch East India Company to the London Company, dated July —, 1620, a translation of which is preserved in the <em>Java Records</em>, vol. ii. pt. 1.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-227" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-227" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ceylon. See p. 124.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-228" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-228" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The letter mentioned above says : ‘The pynnace of Denmarke called the <em>Horison</em> [Oeresund], sent before their fleet, was arrived neer to Negapatnam on the Coast of Coromandell, and being sett upon by six Portugall frigates, who cutt ther rudder in peeces, by which losse being without stay, they chased her on shore at Caricol and spoyled her, in whom they found 3000 realls of eight. The other four shipps of Denmarke were said to be arryved at Ceylon, but the newes were very uncertaine.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-229" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-229" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Willoughby's signature is only a matter of form. Biddulph writes in the first person singular throughout.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-230" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-230" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Senend [probably Sirhind] in the Kinges Lascar, 200 courses from Agra and 100 courses short of Lahore.'</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-231" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-231" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shāhnawāz Khān (see note on p. 60).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-232" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-232" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the Khānkhānān.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-233" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-233" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Pargana</em>, i. e. the tract of country assigned him by the Mogul. ‘Prigonies,’ says Purchas (vol. i. p. 455), ‘are lordships.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-234" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-234" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>cesta</em>, a wicker-basket.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-235" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-235" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 135.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-236" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-236" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>qanāt</em>, a screen placed round a tent or enclosure.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-237" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-237" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Afzal Khān (see note on p. 36).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-238" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-238" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jamāl Khān (see p. 150).</span></li></ol></section>
